Quadrant coordinates are always single digits (1-8), so the guard string `" Quadrant 8 - 8 "` is the exact length of what gets printed, never an approximation.

```
=== 19 ===            === 18 ===            === 12 ===
lq Quadrant 3 - 2 k   lq Quadrant qqqqqk    lqqqqqqqqqqk
x     1 2 3 4 5 6&gt;x   x     1 2 3 4 5 &gt;x    x     1 2 &gt;x
```

- *By exhaustion of the assignment sites.* All eleven places that write `shield` (`setup.c:239,263`, `moving.c:245`, `sst.c:890`, `battle.c:194,209,217,620,978`, `events.c:541,545`) keep `0 &lt;= shield &lt;= inshld`, and `inshld` is only ever 1250.0 or 2500.0. The shield-transfer path clamps to `inshld` explicitly (`battle.c:187-194`), the hit path clamps the absorbed amount to `shield` (`battle.c:619`), and the one unclamped subtraction, `shield -= hit*0.2` in `checkshctrl()` (`battle.c:978`), cannot go negative because `hit = rpow*shield/inshld` and `rpow` is rejected unless it is strictly less than `energy &lt;= inenrg`, so the subtraction is at most `0.4*shield`. Hence `%.1f` is at most `2500.0` (6 characters), the percentage is at most `100` (4 with the sign), and `DAMAGED,` (8) is the longest of the three state words — 40 exactly.
- *By brute force.* I compiled `tuifmt.c` against a scratch driver outside the repository and swept every line over the extremes of `d.date`, `energy`, `shield`, `inshld`, `lsupres`, `d.remkl`, `d.remtime`, `warpfac`, `torps`, both damage flags, `shldup`, `iscloaked`, docked/undocked and all three conditions, including deliberately out-of-range values (negative energy, negative reserves, 99999.9 stardate, 999 Klingons, 999.99 time left). Widest per line: 21, 29, 28, 37, 18, 22, 16, **40**, 17, 20. Line 8 is the only one that reaches 40, and it needs pathological inputs elsewhere to be beaten — none of which the game can produce. On screen at 72x24 the `DAMAGED` line ends `... 2500.0 unitsx`, flush against the border, no marker; at 71 it clips to `... 2500.0 uni&gt;`. So the supported minimum is safe, with exactly zero slack, which is what the comment now says and what #89 records.

<result>## Verdict

Two of the three are right. The recovery paragraph is now accurate and reads as the conjunction you intended, and the test's positional check lands on the correct row and is not timing-sensitive. ## Answers to your two questions

**1. "above about thirty columns and fourteen rows" — the join is right.** It reads as a conjunction, not a disjunction. "so long as the squeeze stopped above about thirty columns and fourteen rows" states two conditions the squeeze must satisfy together, and "Below either, the display does not recover" then names the disjunction explicitly for the failure side, which reinforces the reading rather than muddying it. It does not promise recovery in any case that lacks it. That is the failure of the old wording gone.

One nit, not worth blocking on: "above ... fourteen rows" strictly excludes fourteen, and fourteen is the last size that recovers (`80x14 → 80x24` restores the panels and the prompt; `80x13` does not). The same seam exists at thirty columns, but "about" covers that one and there is no "about" governing the rows. Since the paragraph above sets the cliff precisely — "Shorter than fourteen rows" — a reader who compares the two gets the right answer anyway. If you want it airtight, "stopped at about thirty columns and fourteen rows or better" closes it.

**"Quitting and starting again is the way out; it works even when you cannot see it" matches my measurement, and not naming the sequence is the right call.** What I reached the farewell with was `quit`, `n`, Enter, `n` — but that is one path through the ending, not the path: the answers depend on whether you want the score recorded and whether you want to play again, and a player at a different point in a game may meet a different set. A README that named it would be stating a sequence that is only sometimes the sequence. "It works even when you cannot see it" is the load-bearing fact and it is true.

**2. The pager sentence over-claims at fifteen — should-fix, `README.md:34-36`.** I paged `lrscan` all the way through at both sizes, TUI engaged by starting at 100x30 and resizing down (starting at 80x15 directly does not reach the TUI at all — it refuses and falls back, which is why this has to be measured as a resize).

At **fourteen** rows nothing is readable at any point. Six presses, six identical screens:

```
mqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqjmqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqj
 [HIT SPACE BAR TO CONTINUE]
```

then `COMMAND&gt;`. "A scan pages past with nothing to read" is exactly right for fourteen. (It also corrects something I said in round 1: it is not endless — it ends after as many pages as the scan has lines, having shown none of them. Worth having in #95 if that phrasing went in.)

At **fifteen** the scan is fully readable, one line per page — the message window has two rows, one for content and one for the pager, and `LINES-PANELH-1` is 1 rather than 0:

```
page 0:  (blank)                              page 3:      7    5   16
         [HIT SPACE BAR TO CONTINUE]                   [HIT SPACE BAR TO CONTINUE]
page 1:  Long-range scan for Quadrant 3 - 2   page 4:      1    3    9
         [HIT SPACE BAR TO CONTINUE]                   [HIT SPACE BAR TO CONTINUE]
page 2:      4    4    7                      page 6:  COMMAND&gt;
```

Heading, all three data rows, then back to the prompt. Cramped and slow, but a player gets the scan. Lumping it with fourteen tells them a size that works does not. My round-1 report is where this came from — I measured fifteen with a single capture and reported "one blank line", which was the first page, not the whole story. Suggested fix, keeping your structure:

&gt; Just above that there is barely more — at fourteen rows the one line left over goes entirely to the pager, so a scan pages past with nothing to read at all; at fifteen it comes one line at a time.

If #95 says fifteen shows nothing, it is worth a correcting comment there too, since the sentence and the issue came from the same measurement.

## Blocker/should-fix: my suggested positive control swallows the assertion it was meant to protect

`/home/scottdensmore/Developer/scottdensmore/super-star-trek/tests/tui.sh:1136-1143`

The caption is drawn at row 0, column 2. The title is drawn at row 0, column 2. At one row they are the same sixteen columns, so a build with the guard relaxed erases `Quadrant` with the caption — which means the control fails, the `elif` never runs, and the block reports a defect that does not exist while staying silent about the one that does.

Not inferred. I rebuilt the `&gt;= 1` mutant from the current sources and ran the current test against it:

```
FAIL: sensors: the panel is not drawn at all at one row
```

Before the control was added, that same mutant reported `sensors: the caption was drawn over the only row there is`. The control made the diagnosis worse. This is the #64 shape AGENTS.md step 4 names — an assertion tripping whose text points elsewhere — and I introduced it, so I am not going to approve past it.

The control has to be something the caption cannot overwrite. Non-blankness is enough, it is what the control actually needs to establish, and it is render-agnostic (`uniform_col`'s comment already warns that capture-pane renders the line-drawing set as it pleases, so a control keyed on `q` runs would be worse):

```sh
		# The positive control first: absence proves nothing on a
		# screen where nothing was painted, and this assertion is
		# nothing but an absence. It cannot ask for the title --
		# the caption is drawn over exactly where the title is, so
		# a build with the guard relaxed would fail the control and
		# never reach the line that names the defect.
		if ! screen | head -1 | grep -q '[^ ]'; then
			fail "sensors: nothing at all was drawn at one row"
			dump
		elif screen | grep -qF 'SENSORS DAMAGED'; then
			fail "sensors: the caption was drawn over the only row there is"
			dump
		fi
```

Verified both ways, on fresh out-of-tree copies with their own build directories:

- `&gt;= 1` mutant with this control: `FAIL: sensors: the caption was drawn over the only row there is` — the accurate message, and the only one.
- Unmutated build with this control: `tui OK`.

Keeping `if`/`elif` is right, and your reason for not using `unwanted` holds — the control must run first. That answer only breaks down if the control is one the defect can erase, which is exactly what happened. The mutant capture, for the record:

```
mq SENSORS DAMAGED qqqqqqqqqjmq Status qqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqqj
```

## The should-fix: confirmed, and stronger than argued

The replacement is right and your verification of the spacing premise matches mine (`fmt_quad_line` writes `"%2d "` then `" %c"`; header is `"    1 2 …"`, one space). I did not just re-run your seeds — I took the actual broken capture and substituted the cell the old pattern leaned on:

| capture | old pattern | new pattern |
|---|---|---|
| `mq 9 … .10 .` (real mutant) | MATCH | MATCH |
| same with `.10 *` | **quiet** | MATCH |
| healthy 72x11 | quiet | quiet |

That is the demonstration the comment claims, done against the thing itself rather than against three galaxies that happened to agree. The guard-removed mutant still fails `short: two grid rows were drawn onto one line` under the current test, and the fixed build is `tui OK`.

**On the galaxy shape you asked about: I cannot construct one, and I think none exists.** The reasoning is closed rather than sampled:

- The second label is always preceded by the previous row's last *cell*, and `quad` cells are never digits (`.` `*` `E` `K` `C` `S` `B` `P` `@` `?` `#` `-` and blank), so `[^0-9]` always holds.
- Only grid row 10 can ever be the appended one — row 11 is the first `wmove` that fails — so the label is `10`, which `1?[0-9]` matches; and `1?[0-9]` covers 1-9 too if the collision ever moved.
- A blank first cell only *adds* spaces after the label, so `[0-9]+  ` still holds.
- The header cannot match under `^`: after `x` and its spaces the only digit reachable is the leading `1`, and it is followed by one space.
- Title and border rows fail at `[0-9]+` (`Q`, `q`).

The remaining exposure is a change to `fmt_quad_line`'s format string, not to a galaxy — and that would break the test loudly at 80x24 first.

## The README nit: confirmed

"A terminal shorter than the panels themselves loses rows off the bottom of them" is accurate — the panels are 13 deep and rows first drop at 12 — and it no longer implies anything about 24. The rest of that section I re-verified against the running game in the first pass and it still stands.

## `cut -c1-29` — fine implied, one clause better

Your read is correct: 29 is `QUADW`, the quadrant window is 29 wide at any terminal ≥ 29 columns because `resize_term` only shrinks, and the `-x 72` three lines above pins it. `cut -c1-29` takes 1-indexed columns 1-29 = the window's 0-indexed columns 0-28, which stops exactly one column short of `wstat`'s left border — I checked that boundary against a real capture, and it is right, not right-by-luck.

I would name the number, because the failure mode of getting it wrong is quiet in the wrong direction: a narrower size added to this block readmits status-panel digits, which turns a clean assertion into a *spurious* failure that a future maintainer is likely to fix by loosening the pattern. Since you are editing the file anyway, one clause on the existing sentence does it — "…the status panel beside it is full of digits. Twenty-nine is `QUADW`, which the panel keeps at any width at or above it, and this only ever runs at 72." Not worth a round on its own; fold it in with the control fix.

## I took the invitation, and it found one

You were right that this was the moment. **`tests/tui.sh:989-991` — `grep -qF 'Time Left'` had never been observed failing**: it sits behind the `elif` of the grid-row check, and every mutant run so far — mine and the verifier's — tripped the first branch and short-circuited past it. FAIL: short: the last status line is missing at 80x24

| `Time Left` | `panel_has_row(wstat, row+1)` | **last status line missing at 80x24** (new) |
| sweep border awk ×3 | guards deleted | drawn onto the bottom border at 72x12/11/9 |
| two-labels regex | guards deleted | two grid rows on one line |
| `NR == 11` / `NR == 2` caption | `PANELH-1` | lost its reason / not on the border |
| 80x1 control + negative | `&gt;= 1` | caption drawn over the only row |
| part 2 | `wresize(wstat, PANELH, …)` | drawn onto the bottom border |

## One nit — and explicitly *not* the shape you are hunting

**`tests/tui.sh:987` — `'^x 10 '` is the only assertion in the suite that depends on how `capture-pane` renders the line-drawing set.** The file's own `uniform_col` comment establishes the opposite convention: "Which character it is does not matter — capture-pane renders the line-drawing set as it pleases." The `x` is ACS_VLINE's ASCII fallback.

It is safe today for a reason I checked rather than assumed: nothing in the tree calls `setlocale()`, so ncurses runs in the C locale and emits ACS escapes, which tmux stores and replays as `x`/`q` — on macOS by the same reasoning. **And its failure mode is a spurious FAIL, never a silent pass**, so it cannot hide a defect. That is the opposite of the three assertions this branch has already produced, and I do not want the framing of your question to push it up a severity it does not deserve.

If you want it render-agnostic anyway, `'^. 10  '` is a verified drop-in — I ran all three candidates against a real 80x24 capture:

| pattern | lines matched |
|---|---|
| `^x 10 ` | 1 (grid row 10) |
| `^. 10  ` | 1 (grid row 10) |
| ` 10  ` | **3** — header, `Torpedoes     10`, grid row 10 |

The third is the trap: dropping the anchor to escape the `x` would silently weaken the check to something the column header satisfies on its own. `^. 10  ` keeps the selectivity because the header has one space after its leading `1` where a grid row has two — the same spacing fact the two-labels regex now rests on. Your call; leaving it is defensible, and I would not hold the commit for it.

## Everything else stands

The judgment calls (silent row-clipping, dropping from the bottom, part 2 belonging to #91 rather than #88, the 80x1/80x2 sizes as sound test practice), the closed argument that no galaxy shape fools the two-labels regex, the `-Werror` build, 11/11 `ctest`, and the README's four numeric claims measured against the running game.

Nothing further from me. The pass coverage claim holds: `ui-review` saw the last player-visible state, and everything since has been test-file material that I have now exercised directly.</result>

## Your two questions

**1. Dangling promise — no, with one residue.** I grepped: nothing in `AGENTS.md` now mentions `.claude/settings.json`, `enabledPlugins`, or a clone having anything. Compression that keeps all four and answers both, one clause instead of two sentences:

&gt; …and where the change stands with the adversarial pass below: answered (and whether anything has changed since), declined, still to ask — normally still to ask, since `code-review` comes first — or nobody to ask.

So your new wording is consistent with the `verifier`'s precedent, and `code-review` is the odd one out. That makes it a small, well-founded change to step 8 — its own branch and commit.

## Your first question: is the message still claiming more than it should?

No. It is now the narrowest true statement of the relationship, and I would not change it.

**On pointers versus the message — the message was the better fix.** Four reasons, and I hold this fairly firmly:

- Step 6's pointer already scopes itself to all three passes. `the standard that "Answering a finding" below sets for all three passes` is a forward reference on behalf of 7 and 8; it is located in step 6, but a numbered workflow is read in order, and step 6 is where a reader first meets the standard.
- The back-link exists too. The section opens `Steps 6, 7 and 8 hold their passes to one standard` (line 386), so a reader arriving at the section knows its scope without being sent there from 7 or 8.
- Adding two pointers means adding two more assertions about how the steps and the section relate — which is the class of statement that has now been wrong twice in this file within one change. Each cross-reference is another thing that goes stale under renumbering, and renumbering is precisely #65's subject. Fewer such assertions, each verified, beats three of them.
- It would lengthen steps 7 and 8 to convey nothing a reader of step 6 does not already have.

The one wording seam I noticed is not worth a round: step 6 says the section *sets* the standard, the test message says it is where the standard is *defined*. Compatible, and neither is wrong.

## Your second question: should #98 have been fixed here?

**Your call stands. Do not pull it in.** I tested the argument for reversing it rather than weighing it in the abstract, and its premise does not hold.

The claim is that a file failing open in three places is not worth a fourth check. But none of the three can mask the new check. `agentsbytes`/`claudebytes` come from `wc -c` and `claudelines` from `grep -cv`; the finding count comes from `awk`. Three independent captures, three independent comparisons. I demonstrated this last round in the run where `awk` and `grep` were both unavailable: the new guard fired with `could not count the finding standard ... so this check did not run` and the script exited 1, while line 136's `[: Illegal number:` scrolled past in the same run doing nothing. The new check is strictly additive and inherits none of their weakness — so the fourth check makes the file better in exactly the place it is added, whatever the other three do.

Against pulling it in, three things:

- `AGENTS.md` step 9 is directly on point: *"Do not fix it in this slice. An unrelated fix buried in an unrelated diff is how both the fix and the finding go unreviewed. Fix it here only if it belongs to the slice."* The boundary it draws is the one you drew — the three sites are pre-existing on `main` (I confirmed via `git show main:tests/instructions.sh`), the fourth was fixed here because this change created it.
- Step 4 would bind on the three fixes too, and proving them needs the shadowed-binary harness that #98 itself identifies as absent from the tree. Landing three guards with no failing-first proof is the failure mode step 4 exists to prevent, and building that harness is a slice of its own.
- It would roughly double a docs-alignment slice and move its center away from what #97 asked for.

#98 is well-written and its line references are accurate against the current file. One note for whoever picks it up, not a finding here: its acceptance criteria bundle the helper with the harness decision, and if the harness proves expensive the three guards are still worth landing without it rather than stalling behind it.

## On the lead you changed beyond what I asked

You were right and my suggestion was the worse of the two. Keeping "Commit after approval." and adding a body clause saying approval is not always required leaves the contradiction on the line an agent skimming twelve steps actually reads, and puts the correction where only a careful reader finds it — the defect one line up, as you say. No change wanted.

## On three checks over one section

Keep all three. Two points that change the arithmetic you were weighing:

The concentration is **two literals, not three**. The count check depends on `STANDARD`, a different string; only its *message* mentions the section by name, and a stale message is a cosmetic staleness, not a failing check. So renaming the section is a **two-file** edit — `AGENTS.md` heading plus pointer, `tests/instructions.sh` two greps — not four.

And the mutants show the two are not the same assertion: one catches a dangling pointer (heading renamed out from under it), the other catches an orphaned section (pointer deleted). Dropping either loses a distinct one-place regression, which is the class this check exists for. That is a different trade from the cross-references I argued against last round: those were assertions in *prose* that go stale invisibly, where these fail loudly and name themselves.

If you want the concentration reduced without giving up a check, hoist the name the way you hoisted `STANDARDS`:

```sh
SECTION='Answering a finding'
```

then `grep -q "^### $SECTION"` and `grep -q "\"$SECTION\""`. Renaming becomes one line in the script. Optional — I would take it, but it is not a condition of approval.

### 1. The corrected mechanism — right, and I measured the two ways it could have been wrong

I probed ncurses with three windows on one screen: A 13x29 at column 0, B 13x29 at column 51 (right edge flush against column 79 at 80 columns), C 13x20 at column 10.

| screen | A | B | C |
|---|---|---|---|
| 80 → 60 | 29 (untouched) | 29 (untouched, now hanging off the right edge) | 20 |
| 60 → 80 | 29 | 29 | 20 |
| 80 → 25 | **25** | **25** | 20 (untouched, hanging off) |
| 25 → 80 | **80** | **80** | 20 |

- **It is spanning, not touching an edge.** B sat flush against the right edge at 80 columns and the 80→60 squeeze ignored it entirely. Position never mattered in either direction: B, at column 51 and wholly off-screen at 25 columns, grew to 80 exactly as A did.
- **It is spanning, not "no longer fits at its position" either.** C hung nine columns off the right edge at 25 columns and was left alone, because its *width* still fitted the screen.

So the grow-back trigger the comment names — a window as wide as the screen follows the screen — is exactly the rule. The one compression is the "down" half: what gets cut down is a window *wider than the screen*, which is what makes it span in the first place. Your next clause, "a panel squeezed past its own size stops being 29 columns and starts tracking the terminal", already states that condition precisely, so the sentence as a whole predicts the symptom the test asserts. No change needed.

Worth knowing but not worth writing down: this is also why `wquad` was the only casualty. `statw` is `max(COLS-QUADW,1)` and `msgw` is `max(COLS-2,1)`, so neither is ever wider than the screen and curses never resizes them behind the code's back; `wmsg`'s only mistreatment is the `begy` shift, which is the separate below-the-bottom rule your second comment covers on its own. The two comments already split the two mechanisms correctly.

### 2. The README qualifier is in the right place

Keep it where it is. It qualifies the one sentence that can fail, and a reader who stops after the second paragraph would otherwise carry away an absolute promise the fifth paragraph then contradicts — which was the whole finding. It does not hedge the ordinary case, because in the ordinary case the condition is met and the sentence reads as the promise it was: the paragraph immediately below ("Dragged smaller than the 72x24 the panels ask for…") is what tells the reader when room runs out, so "still room for them" resolves within a few lines rather than dangling. Moving it down to the deep-squeeze paragraph would restore the contradiction; dropping it would too.

The only thing I would even raise is rhythm, and it is optional: the clause currently interrupts the promise before it is made ("and so long as there is still room for them, both the question … survive"). Trailing it — "and both the question you were asked and the answer you were halfway through typing survive, so long as there is still room for them" — keeps #46 and #79's feature in the main clause and the condition as a caveat. Take it or leave it; both are accurate.

In an outside-the-repo copy I capped the quadrant panel at 20 columns on resize; the block reported `restore: after 25x24 the quadrant panel came back the wrong width` and the same at 20x8 — the two cases where the screen had actually been narrow, which is where a too-narrow restore can arise. ## F1 — fixed, and the check has teeth

| mutant | result |
|---|---|
| baseline copy | `instructions OK`, exit 0 |
| **step 6's pointer clause removed, the Codex section's quoted mention left in place** | **exit 1**, "nothing in AGENTS.md points forward at…" |
| Codex section's quoted mention removed, step 6's kept | exit 0 (correct — a later mention is optional) |
| both quoted mentions removed | exit 1 |
| heading renamed | exit 1 on the separate heading check |

Your reproduction of my mutant is confirmed. Portability: identical pass/fail under `sh`, `dash`, `bash`, `bash --posix`, `busybox sh`, and with `awk` forced to `mawk` and `busybox awk` (no `gawk` or `original-awk` on this box — environment gap, and the program uses only `index`, concatenation and `exit &lt;ternary&gt;`, all POSIX). `shellcheck tests/instructions.sh` is clean, exit 0. `ctest -R instructions` passes; three consecutive direct runs pass; no flakes.

**Your question 1 — is "pointers point forward" right in principle?** It is safe here, and it does not forbid a legitimate later reference (mutant 3 above passes with the Codex section's mention present). Two things it is not, which the comment slightly oversells:

- **It checks "something above", not "a numbered step".** I moved step 6's pointer into `### The review sub-agents` — above the heading, but not in a numbered step — and the check passes. The comment directly above it still says it guards "the one thing tying the numbered steps to the definition." An anchor on the *first* `### ` heading rather than on the target heading would say what the comment says; as written it says something weaker. Not a defect, a scope mismatch between code and comment.
- **It encodes file order as the proxy.** I relocated the whole `### Answering a finding` section above `## Development workflow`, leaving step 6's pointer intact and correct, and the check goes red. So the rule is wrong in exactly one layout: the definition sitting above the steps that point at it. That layout is unlikely here and partly foreclosed already — the heading check hard-requires `### Answering a finding`, so promoting it to a `##` near the top fails first — and when it does misfire it fails loudly with a message naming the cause. On balance: keep it. The risk of a spurious red is small and self-explaining; the hole it closes was silent.

One thing you may want anyway: **a rewrap breaks it.** I split the pointer across two lines (`"Answering a\n   finding"`) and it fails, blaming a missing pointer that is present. The count check five lines above flattens the file for exactly this reason, and its comment argues "a check a rewrap can break is a check that gets deleted the first time someone rewraps." The two neighbouring checks now use opposite conventions. This is not a regression — the old `grep -q` had the same fragility — but this diff is the moment it was rewritten. A flattened stream with a "have I passed the heading yet" flag would give both properties.

Also noted, not blocking: with `awk` unavailable both awk-based checks go red (safe direction), but the count check reports "awk exited non-zero, so this check did not run" while the new one reports "nothing points forward at…" — a misleading diagnosis for a true failure. It cannot hide a defect, so I would leave it.

### Findings

**should-fix — `AGENTS.md:454`: "GitHub allows one reaction per user per issue" is not true, and the paragraph two above it depends on it not being true.**
GitHub allows each reaction *type* once per user per issue — one user can hold 👍 and 👀 and ❤️ simultaneously; re-POSTing an existing one returns the existing reaction rather than creating a second. As written, the rule says the bot cannot show 👀 and 👍 at the same time, which the section at `AGENTS.md:439-441` describes it as doing in sequence and says nothing about it withdrawing. The conclusion you draw is unaffected — a 👍 still cannot be repeated — so this is one clause: "GitHub allows each reaction only once per user per issue".

**should-fix — `AGENTS.md:455-457`: the load-bearing claim is deduced, and the paragraph that sorts measured from read does not say so.**
"A pull request that drew one on opening will still show that same 👍 after a fix push, `created_at` and all, whether or not anything reviewed the push" is an inference from the mechanics above, not an observation: neither #99 nor #102 exercised 👍-on-open-then-push (#99's first pass posted comments and no reaction; #102 had one pass and no second push), and it holds only if the bot does not remove and re-add its own reaction, which nobody here has watched it do. That inference is what step 12 now rests on, and `AGENTS.md:482-489` enumerates what is measured and what is read without a place for a third category. A clause — "unless it withdraws and re-adds the reaction, which has not been seen here" — puts the claim on the same footing as everything around it. This is the one remaining sentence in the section that outruns its evidence.

**nit — `AGENTS.md:453, 463`: the reaction paragraph is in the right place and the wrong typeface.**
You asked whether it reads as the subject. It does, and the cause is mechanical rather than editorial: it opens with a bold lead-in, so a reader scanning bold sees three items under a banner that says "Two things to know about it." Un-bold the first sentence and leave the paragraph exactly where it is — it qualifies the reactions described above it and the "let the review run again" below it, so it sits between them correctly. The section then reads as four prose paragraphs, a two-item list that really has two items, and the provenance close. On length: at nine lines it is no longer conspicuously the longest — "It reads this file" and the provenance paragraph are eight each — so I would not move it, only stop it announcing itself.

**nit — `tests/instructions.sh:145-146`: two consecutive bare `#` lines** where the separator went in; the neighbouring paragraph breaks use one.

## Findings

### Should-fix

**A. `AGENTS.md:494-495` — "a push can start a fresh review" is filed as measured, but the single observation behind it is confounded.**

The only run that produced it is #99, and the timeline is:

- 00:14:35 PR opened; 00:18:32 bot commenting review on `ac5ebf2754`, the opening head — that is the *open* trigger, not a push.
- 00:56:42 second commit `6ce7309c` pushed.
- 00:57:22 and 00:57:23 two author thread replies.
- 00:59:44 bot 👍.

#102 had one commit and no push after opening; #104 has had no push at all. So the sole datum is a 👍 three minutes after a push that was also forty seconds before two replies. Both candidate causes precede the one effect, and the bot's own note — cited two sentences later — lists neither a plain reply nor a push, only `@codex review`. Which of the two it answered was not watched.

Failure scenario: a reader takes the measured category at face value, pushes a fix, and waits for a re-review that may have been reply-triggered and may not come — the exact wait step 12 tells them to bound.

Fix: narrow the claim to what was seen, e.g. "that something after a push starts a fresh review — #99 drew a 👍 three minutes after one, though two thread replies landed forty seconds after that push too, so which it answered is not something we watched." "Within six hours of each other" is timezone-free and verifiable from the same API call.

Three sections later the file says the bot's note lists its triggers — and one of exactly three is `Comment "@codex review"`. A reader holding both gets a contradiction out of one document.

**A. `AGENTS.md:500-503` — "the pull request now reads as though nothing ever reacted to it" is a present-tense claim about a live pull request, and this file's own measured claim predicts the next push will falsify it.**

Six lines above, the file records that something after a push starts a fresh review, evidenced by #99 drawing a 👍 three minutes after one. #104 is open, and pushing these fixes is the next thing that happens to it. If the bot runs again and finds nothing, it reacts 👍 — and #104 stops reading as though nothing ever reacted to it, while the file says it does. The sentence is falsifiable by the commit that carries it.

The durable half of the point is the first half. Fix by keeping that and dropping the state claim: "…that one is already unrecoverable, since GitHub keeps no history of a reaction taken back." **C. `AGENTS.md:464` — "this file's own pull request" is deictic and goes stale on merge; the other mention of the same event already uses the durable form.**

Line 500 cites `#104`. Line 464 calls the same pull request "this file's own", which is only true while this is the open one. ## Your two questions

**1. Is the measured paragraph readable? No — split it, as you said you'd rather do.**

The honesty is right and worth keeping; the sentence carrying it is not. Two claims, each with a subordinate tail (the push confound; the unrecoverability), joined by a sentence-initial "And that", closing on a "Neither" whose antecedent is eight lines back and a clause that belongs to the first item. A reader who starts it will finish somewhere other than where the paragraph meant them to be.

The split that fixes B at the same time is one claim per paragraph, with its own evidence and its own caveat attached:

&gt; What is measured here rather than read, and by which pull request.
&gt;
&gt; That something after a push starts a fresh review: #99 drew a 👍 three minutes after one. Two thread replies landed forty seconds after that push as well, so which of them it answered is not something anyone watched — and the bot's own note lists its triggers without a push among them, so this is the claim least safe of the two.
&gt;
&gt; That it takes a reaction away again: #104's 👀 went as soon as it had comments to post. GitHub keeps no history of a reaction taken back, so that observation is already unrecoverable, which is the reason to have written it down.
&gt;
&gt; Neither is on the documentation page.

Same facts, nothing floating, and "Neither" now has two short paragraphs behind it instead of one long sentence. It also makes future maintenance obvious: a third observation is a third paragraph, not a re-tallied clause.

**2. Should #104 be in the file? Yes — the number, not the deixis, and not its current reaction state.**

Citing it is exactly right, and it is the strongest citation in the section precisely because it is the one that cannot be re-derived: withdrawn reactions leave nothing behind, so the file is the only record that the event happened. A number in a merged file is not an odd self-reference; it is a footnote, and it stays checkable for the parts that persist — the comment at 05:49:47 is still there and still dated.

What reads oddly after merge is the other two forms of it: "this file's own pull request" (finding C), which only makes sense while #104 is open, and "now reads as though nothing ever reacted to it" (finding A), which asserts a mutable state that will very likely change before anyone else reads the sentence. Fix those two and the self-reference stops being a self-reference and becomes a citation, which is what you want it to be.

## On the push being the test of the claim

You are right that it is, and I'd rather you hear the design flaw now: **as currently planned, the experiment reproduces the exact confound the file already documents.** Your stated sequence is commit, push, reply on the Codex thread and resolve it. That is a push and thread comments within a minute or two of each other — which is #99, precisely. If a fresh review runs, you will not know whether it answered the push or the reply, and the paragraph at 497-501 will have a second observation that says nothing its caveat does not already say.

Separating them costs nothing and converts it into a real measurement:

1. Push. Note the time.
2. Wait the few minutes a review takes, touching nothing else on the pull request. Watch for a new review, new comments, and a reaction appearing on #104.
3. Only then reply on the thread and resolve it.

A review that arrives in step 2 is attributable to the push alone, since nothing else happened — that is the observation #99 could not give you, and it would let the hedge at 500-501 come down honestly rather than by accumulation.

Two cautions on reading the result:

- **A null is weak.** The section already says absence is not something the platform reports; a service on infrastructure this repository does not control can be slow, throttled, or down. One quiet push is not evidence that a push does not trigger a review, and the paragraph should not be reversed on it. If you record a null, record it as a null — "a push on #104 drew nothing within N minutes" — not as a correction to the claim. The structure you now have supports that: a third observation is a third paragraph.
- **Watch the reaction too, not just the comments.** If the bot 👍s #104 after the push, that is a reaction added to a pull request whose 👀 it had previously withdrawn. It does not show a 👍 being taken back, so line 467's "whether it would take a 👍 back is unwatched" stays true — but add-then-remove-then-add on one pull request is a further data point about reaction management, and it is worth writing down while it is observable, since as line 505 now says, none of this survives in the API afterwards.

Your commitment to report what actually happened rather than what you expected is the right one, and it is the reason the "already unrecoverable" sentence earns its place.

## What I could not review

Whether the published documentation page says what the section says it does not say — I have the bot's own review-footer note verbatim from both #99 and #104, but not the docs page behind the settings link, which is why finding A matters more than its size suggests.</result>

- `+1` on issue 104 at `2026-08-09T06:36:41Z` — matches, and it is the only reaction present.
- Push candidate: `50540ea` committer date `2026-08-09T06:33:53Z`, i.e. 24 s before the claimed 06:34:17Z push. Consistent.

The skip was stated to me as step 8 requires. `verifier` running in parallel is noted; my approval, when it comes, will not cover a state changed by its findings.

## Findings

### should-fix

**1. `AGENTS.md:323-324` — step 12 promises the section says "when to stop waiting", and after the rewrite it no longer does.**
The deleted paragraph carried the only duration in the document: "Give it the few minutes a review takes." What replaced it says how to *read* the answer and nothing about how long to wait; the only remaining stopping guidance is "Where the answer still does not come", which is a condition with no clock in it. An agent following step 12 literally has no bound and will either poll indefinitely or invent one. The measurements are right there to supply it: 👀 at +5 s, 👍 at +2 m 24 s on #104, and the opening review at +3 m 39 s (#104 created 05:46:08Z, review 05:49:47Z). Suggest restoring a sentence such as "A review lands within a few minutes — five seconds to the 👀, under four to the answer on both runs watched here; past that, treat it as not coming."

**2. `AGENTS.md:515-516` — "Comparing `created_at` against the push is what makes that not matter" overstates what the comparison buys.**
The comparison rules out a *false positive* (mistaking a stale 👍 for a fresh one). It does nothing about the failure mode the preceding two sentences just raised: if the bot does not withdraw an existing 👍 to start the next cycle, GitHub's one-reaction-per-user rule means no new `created_at` can ever appear, step 12's condition becomes permanently unsatisfiable, and every merge on such a PR goes through the escape hatch. That is the opposite of "not mattering" — it is the case where the rule stops working, and the text tells a reader not to worry about it. Suggest: "Comparing `created_at` against the push is what keeps a stale 👍 from being read as a fresh one. If the bot turns out not to withdraw it, no fresh one can arrive at all, and that is the case step 12 hands to `@codex review` and then to whoever merges."

**3. `AGENTS.md:503-504` — "the rule in step 12 rests on that, and it was reasoned before it was watched" contradicts the sentence after it, in the one block whose job is separating reasoned from measured.**
The nearest antecedent of "it" is "the rule in step 12", and that reading is false: step 12's 👍/`created_at` rule is being introduced by this very rewrite, i.e. after the observation, not before it. The intended reading is presumably "this question was settled by reasoning before anyone watched it" — but the next sentence says the reasoning reached the *opposite* conclusion, so "it was reasoned" (which normally means "correctly concluded") lands wrong either way. Suggest dropping the clause and letting the next sentence carry it: "That a 👍 can postdate the push it answers, from the same run — the rule in step 12 rests on it. An earlier draft reasoned the opposite, that a 👍 could not be repeated and so would go stale; the run above disproved that."

**4. `AGENTS.md:508` — "Neither" no longer has exactly two antecedents.**
Before the rewrite the block held two single-claim paragraphs. Now paragraph one bundles two findings ("That a push starts a fresh review, **and** that the reaction cycles") and paragraph two adds a third, so "Neither" covers two paragraphs but three claims. It also partly duplicates work paragraph one already did ("this is behaviour the vendor does not document"). Suggest splitting the reaction-cycling claim into its own paragraph and writing "None of these is on the documentation page", or moving the trigger claim's undocumented-ness entirely into the "Neither" sentence so paragraph one states only the observation.

**5. `AGENTS.md:467-470` — a fourth measured claim sits outside the block that claims to enumerate what is measured.**
"A pass that finds nothing files no review at all — no review object, no comments" is a measurement (I confirmed it: no review object exists for the 06:36:41Z pass), and it is not derivable from the vendor's collapsed note, which only says 👍 means finished with nothing to say. It is never sorted in "What is measured here rather than read", whose header reads as a complete enumeration. Either fold it into the #104 paragraph — it came from the same run — or soften the header.

### nit

**6. `AGENTS.md:496-497` — an inferred timestamp presented as a measurement, an imprecise figure, and a dangling appositive, in three lines.**
GitHub keeps no history of a removed reaction — the deleted draft said so and the rewrite dropped the caveat — so the withdrawal *time* is inferred from when the comments-review posted (05:49:47Z), not observed. The gap to 06:34:22Z is 44 m 35 s, which is three quarters of an hour, not "two thirds", in a paragraph that otherwise quotes seconds. And "— the same 👀 that had been withdrawn…" attaches to the nearest noun phrase, "👍 at 06:36:41Z", so the first reading is that the 👍 *is* the withdrawn 👀. Suggest: "…drew 👀 five seconds later with nothing else touched on the pull request, and 👍 at 06:36:41Z. That 👀 was a re-add: the same reaction had gone when the earlier review posted comments at 05:49:47Z, and GitHub keeps no history of a reaction taken back, which is why it is written down here."

**7. `AGENTS.md:463-465` — the instruction is executable for the reaction but not for the push it is compared against.**
The section names the endpoint that yields `created_at` but never says where the push time comes from, and GitHub does not expose it usefully (`pushedDate` is gone; `git log -1 --format=%cI` gives the commit date, which is only a lower bound). Since the agent doing the pushing is the one asking the question, one clause fixes it: record `date -u +%FT%TZ` at push time. Worth adding too that the reaction's `content` reads `+1` and `eyes`, since an agent writing the `-q` filter needs those literals and 👍 is not spelled 👍 in the JSON.

**8. `AGENTS.md:314-315` vs `494` — "a push **may** start another" against the flat "That a push starts a fresh review".**
One clean instance supports "a push started one here"; step 11 hedges correctly and the provenance block does not, while step 12's gate now depends on the general form holding. Aligning the block to "a push started a fresh review" would cost nothing and keep the three statements from disagreeing.

## What I could not review

- The 👀 at 06:34:22Z and the 👀 withdrawal at 05:49:47Z are unverifiable now — GitHub retains no history of removed reactions, so I could only confirm that they are consistent with the surviving record (a lone `+1` at 06:36:41Z, no reviews or comments between the push and it). Finding 6 is partly about the rewrite having dropped the note that says exactly this.
- Whether the bot's collapsed note publishes "👍 means finished with nothing to say", and whether its trigger list omits pushes — both are vendor text I did not fetch.
- `verifier` scope: it was running in parallel and I have not seen its output, so nothing here reflects it.</result>

## Findings

**should-fix — `AGENTS.md:468-470`: "GitHub stopped serving a push timestamp" is false, and two API calls disprove it with the very number the section cites.**
The section builds a manual bookkeeping rule (`date -u +%FT%TZ` as you push) on the premise that no source exists. Both of these return the push in question:

```
gh api repos/:owner/:repo --jq .pushed_at          -&gt; 2026-08-09T06:34:17Z
gh api 'repos/:owner/:repo/events'                 -&gt; PushEvent refs/heads/docs/codex-pr-review
                                                      2026-08-09T06:34:17Z head 50540eab
```

Failure scenario: an agent that forgot to run `date` — or any later reader checking whether step 12's condition was met on a merge someone else performed — concludes the timestamp is unrecoverable and either skips the check or reports it as unknowable, when one call answers it. The events feed is strictly better than the hand-noted time, because it survives the session. Fix: name `repos/:owner/:repo/events` (filtered on `payload.ref`) as the source, with its real limits — 90 days and 300 events of retention — and keep `repos/:owner/:repo`'s `pushed_at` as the immediately-after-push shortcut with its real limit, that it is repo-wide and any other branch's push clobbers it. Keep `date` as the belt-and-braces note if you like, but not as the only thing that will tell you.

**should-fix — `AGENTS.md:470-473`: the 24-second measurement is attributed to a commit that does not contain the paragraph making it.**
"The commit this paragraph arrived in was made twenty-four seconds before it reached the remote." The paragraph is uncommitted (it appears in `git diff HEAD`, not in `50540ea`). The 24 seconds is real, but it belongs to `50540ea`: committed `2026-08-08T23:33:53-07:00` = `06:33:53Z`, pushed `06:34:17Z`. The commit this paragraph will arrive in has not been made and its delta is unknown. Failure scenario: in a section whose whole organising principle is separating what was measured from what was assumed, a load-bearing number ends up attached to the wrong object — and it is unfalsifiable to the next reader, because the commit it names is whichever one they are looking at. Fix: attribute it, e.g. "`50540ea` was committed twenty-four seconds before it reached the remote".

**should-fix — `tests/instructions.sh:190-194`: the comment implies the new call guards the section's body; it guards one heading line and one quoted pointer.**
"Nothing else in the file guards its body, so this is what stands between a step pointing somewhere and a step pointing nowhere." I replaced everything after `### The Codex review` with `TBD.` in an out-of-tree copy, left the heading and both pointers, and the test exited 0. That is ~110 lines of this branch's main diff still unguarded — the verifier's finding is narrowed, not closed. Failure scenario: the next person reads that comment, believes the body is covered, and does not add the check they were about to. Fix: say what it cannot see, in the register the rest of this file already uses (`instructions.sh:77-84`, `:148-151`) — e.g. "This guards the pointer and the heading, not the body: gut the section to a stub and this still passes." If you want the body covered, a minimum byte or line count between the heading and the next `^### ` is the cheap version, but the honest comment is the required part.

### Nits

- `tests/instructions.sh:155`: `grep -q "^### $name"` interpolates a caller-supplied name into a BRE, while the awk half deliberately uses literal `index()`. Neither current name holds a metacharacter, but `instructions.sh:89-91` warns about exactly this hazard for `STANDARD` and the new function inherits no such note. One line of comment, or `grep -qF` plus a leading-`### ` check, closes it.
- `tests/instructions.sh:196`: `'it is the only place the reviewer steps 11 and 12 defer to is defined'` garden-paths on "the reviewer steps". Both interpolations read grammatically, but "the reviewer that steps 11 and 12 defer to" scans on the first pass.
- `tests/instructions.sh:156`: the folded heading message dropped "step 6 points at it by name" from the old inline version. No coverage lost; the pointer check's message carries the idea.

## The three wrong-reason mechanisms you asked about — all cleared

- **Stale `points` (and now `body`) between calls: no.** I shimmed `awk` on `PATH` to misbehave on each of the 5 call sites × 3 modes (silent-exit-0, prints-plausible-value-but-exits-17, prints junk). **15/15 caught, every one attributed to the right section and the right check.** The `if ! var=$(...)` form reassigns unconditionally, so nothing carries.
- **`index()` with the name in more than one place: handled.** A quoted mention placed *only below* the heading → FAIL (correct: `q &gt; h`). The first-occurrence semantics pick the real forward pointer at line 315 ahead of the heading at 435.
- **Name in a third place: passes, and the script says so.** A quoted `"The Codex review"` inserted anywhere above the heading satisfies the check even with both real step pointers gone. That is explicitly documented ("What it cannot see is which step the pointer is in"). Same for the converse — a duplicate heading above the steps produces a wrong FAIL, also documented. Not defects.

## Gaps found

1. **Prefix-match gap — pre-existing, not introduced here.** Renaming the heading to `### The Codex review process` **passes**. Both `grep -q "^### $name"` and `index(all, "### " s)` match a prefix, so any heading that *starts with* the name satisfies them. I confirmed the same mutation passes under `HEAD`'s script for `Answering a finding`, so the refactor inherited this rather than creating it. Low severity (a reader following the pointer still lands on the section). Under step 9 this is a candidate to file, not to fix in this slice.
2. **One pointer is enough — undocumented.** `The Codex review` has two forward pointers (lines 315 and 323). Unquoting *either one alone* passes; only unquoting both fails. Inherent to an existence check, but it is new that a section has two pointers, and the script's comments don't mention it. Worth a sentence in the comment block or an explicit resolution.

## Missing coverage

- `tests/instructions.sh` has no test of its own; its mutation-resistance is established only by hand (mine and yours). Not new, and not something I'd block on.
- Nothing else. The compiled surface is untouched, and the existing suite covers it.

## Findings

### Should-fix — `AGENTS.md:473-474, 488-490`: the events command as written returns 30 events, not 300, and the next paragraph tells the reader to misread the miss

`gh api repos/:owner/:repo/events` is one page. I measured it just now: `length` is 30, and the oldest entry on that page is `2026-08-09T00:57:22Z` — about 22 hours of this repository's activity. The 300 events the retention sentence promises need `--paginate`; that is how I got the 300 this review rests on.

Failure scenario: a branch open for more than a day or two — `fix/tui-short-terminal`'s `CreateEvent` sits at index 61 of the feed — returns *nothing* from the documented command. The reader then hits AGENTS.md:486-488, which says nothing found means "the feed is behind, not that the push is missing", waits, and re-runs the same one-page call forever. Step 12's condition can never be met, and the reason is a missing flag rather than anything about the bot. The sentence at 488-490 is also literally false of the command given: 300 is what the endpoint *retains*, not what that call returns.

Suggested fix: `gh api --paginate repos/:owner/:repo/events`, and a clause at 488-490 saying the default page is 30, so the three hundred needs `--paginate` — which also makes "three hundred is what this repository returns today" true of the command printed above it.

### Nit — `AGENTS.md:486-491`: the lag paragraph gives the diagnosis but attaches the remedy to the wrong limit

"Note the time yourself as you push if the answer has to outlive either limit" reads as covering the two retention limits in the sentence before it, not the lag in the sentence before that. GitHub documents this endpoint's latency as anywhere from 30 seconds to six hours, which is far past "the few minutes a review takes"; a reader who concludes only "the feed is behind" has been told what is happening and not what to do instead. Suggested fix: extend the remedy to the lag — note the push time yourself rather than waiting the feed out — since the fallback already exists one sentence later.

### What I could verify

### What I could not verify

## Findings

**should-fix — `AGENTS.md:575`. "So no 👍 outlives the push it predates" is a universal drawn from one observation, and it is false as written.**
Three ways it fails. It is contradicted six lines later by its own paragraph: `AGENTS.md:581-584` says the stale 👍 *did* outlive the push, by sixteen seconds. It contradicts the section's own hedge at `AGENTS.md:540` — a push only *may* start a review, and where none starts, nothing withdraws the 👍 and it outlives that push indefinitely. And it sets a standard the same section refuses two paragraphs earlier: at `:539` two instances of the trigger buy only "two instances rather than a promise," while one instance here buys a universal. Failure scenario: an agent reads the summary sentence, trusts that any 👍 it sees post-push is fresh, and skips the `created_at` comparison step 12 rests on. Suggested fix: state the observation, not the law — "once, watched: the bot withdrew a standing 👍 rather than leaving it in place" — and let the operative claim be the weaker one the run actually supports and step 12 actually needs, that a newer 👍 can arrive on a pull request that already carried one.

**should-fix — `AGENTS.md:554`. The push time `23:35:52Z` disagrees with the events feed, which records that push at `23:35:58Z`.**
`gh api --paginate repos/:owner/:repo/events` — the recipe this section prescribes at `:473-475` — returns `2026-08-09T23:35:58Z` for `6ed975b`. The section used that same feed for the other push (`06:34:17Z` matches it exactly), so two pushes are now timed off two different clocks and the file does not say so. Every derived number in the new paragraph moves: 👀 at 16s becomes 10s, 👍 at 3m56s becomes 3m50s, and the sixteen-second window `:582` leans on is six seconds — 38% — of its own size. The section at `:486-500` describes the feed's lag as *delivery* lag, not timestamp skew, which makes `23:35:58Z` the better-supported push time and `23:35:52Z` likely a note taken as the push began. Failure scenario: a reader reproducing the measurement by the section's own instructions gets numbers that disagree with the section and has no way to tell which is wrong — in the one section whose subject is exactly that distinction. Suggested fix: say where `23:35:52Z` came from and give the feed's `23:35:58Z` beside it, or use the feed time throughout and restate the intervals as ten seconds and three minutes fifty.

**should-fix — `AGENTS.md:571-585`. Deleting the "still unwatched" bucket leaves the three-way sort claiming everything is now settled, and it is not.**
The measured/documented/unwatched structure was the section's mechanism for admitting ignorance, and the bucket is not actually empty. Still unwatched: the withdrawal of the 👀 on a *commenting* run — `:547-550` says that one is dated from the comments rather than watched, and that admission now sits inside the "measured" bucket where it reads as measured; whether the bot withdraws a standing 👍 when the review that follows has comments to post, which no run here has produced, since the only commenting run started from no reaction; and whether `@codex review` at `:520-521` triggers anything here, which is documented and never exercised. Failure scenario: the next agent to extend this section has no place to record what it did not see, and the sort quietly stops being a sort. Suggested fix: keep the bucket and repopulate it with those three, naming the push or comment that would settle each — which is what made the deleted paragraph pay for itself.

**should-fix — `AGENTS.md:575-579` against `:562-565`. Two paragraphs now each claim to be the run that killed the staleness theory, in nearly the same words.**
`:562-565` says an earlier draft held "a 👍 could not be repeated and so would go stale — which that run disproved," pointing at 06:34. `:575-579` says "that was the open question in this section until the run above," pointing at 23:35, against a draft that held "a 👍 could never be replaced." These are two different propositions — 06:34 showed a 👍 can arrive where none stood, 23:35 showed one can replace one already standing — and only the second was open after 06:34. As written a reader cannot tell them apart or tell which run settled which. Suggested fix: name the two propositions distinctly in the two paragraphs, and drop "the open question in this section" from the second unless it says which question survived the first run.

**nit — `AGENTS.md:560`. "the only one it ran on itself" is contradicted by the paragraph above it.**
`:536-537` describes the 06:34 push as drawing 👀 "with nothing else touched on the pull request," and `6ed975b`'s own commit message says "Pushing `50540ea` tested it, with nothing else touched on #104." That was equally an experiment the file ran on itself. What is distinctive about 23:35 is that the file predicted it in advance and named it. Suggested fix: "the only one this file called in advance."

**nit — `AGENTS.md:507-510`. "with 👀 up inside twenty seconds each time" reads as covering all three runs, and the first run's 👀 was never timed.**
Grammatically the clause attaches to the two later pushes, which is accurate (five and sixteen seconds), but the sentence opens with "All three runs" and "each time" pulls the first one in. The first run's 👀 has no recorded timestamp anywhere in the section — `:545-550` treats even its existence as inferred from the re-add. Suggested fix: "with 👀 up inside twenty seconds on both of those."

## On step 12

Step 12 asks for a 👍 whose `created_at` postdates your last push; the 23:35 run is the first evidence that such a 👍 can arrive on a pull request already carrying one, which is precisely the case that would have made the condition unsatisfiable. ## On your pushback about #99: you are right, not rationalising

Restore nothing. The API confirms the confound exactly as you describe it — `PushEvent` for `refs/heads/docs/align-finding-standards` head `6ce7309` at `00:57:22Z`, and `pulls/99/reviews` showing `scottdensmore COMMENTED` at `00:57:22Z` (id 4890220381) and `00:57:23Z` (id 4890220407). Three candidate events inside two seconds, one 👍 at `00:59:44Z`.

Three reasons the exclusion is correct, and the second is the one that decides it:

1. It fails the section's own cleanliness criterion, stated in its own words one sentence earlier: "with nothing else touched on the pull request."
2. The obvious counter — "thread replies are not a documented trigger, so they cannot be the cause" — is self-defeating here. I read the bot's note on `pulls/104/reviews/4890633525`: the triggers it lists are opening a PR for review, marking a draft ready, and commenting `@codex review`. A push is not among them either. The whole point of the entry is that the published list is incomplete. You cannot use its incompleteness as evidence for one unlisted candidate and against another. (Neither reply body contains `@codex`; I checked. That rules them out as a *documented* trigger, which is not the question.)
3. Timing separates nothing: 2m22s from the push, 2m21s from the second reply, against an observed answer spread of 2m24s–3m50s.

What you have is a prior — two clean push instances, zero reply instances — which makes the push the likelier explanation and does not make #99 a watched one. That distinction is the entry's entire subject. If anything, the paragraph could earn its exclusion in one more clause by saying *why* a reply is a live candidate (because the trigger list is already known incomplete), which is the sharpest defence against the next reader trying to restore it.

**Should-fix — `AGENTS.md:513-517`: #102 is missing from the survey and breaks the four-minute figure.**
"All four runs timed below answered inside four minutes" surveys the repository's Codex history, but a fifth run is not below and did not. `gh api repos/:owner/:repo/pulls/102` gives `created_at` `2026-08-09T04:39:34Z`; `gh api repos/:owner/:repo/issues/102/reactions` gives a single `+1` from the bot at `2026-08-09T04:45:13Z`. That is 5m39s, with no bot review filed. It was not a draft: the `issues/102/timeline` holds no `ready_for_review` and no `reopened`, only `cross-referenced`, `merged`, `closed`, `head_ref_deleted`. Failure scenario: a reader takes four minutes as the observed ceiling, gives it "a few minutes more" grudgingly, and calls a run dead that in this repository has already once taken 5m39s. Note that the *operational* advice survives — four plus a few is still over 5m39s — so the fix is to the evidence sentence, not the threshold: name #102 and say the observed range is 2m22s to 5m39s.

**Should-fix — `AGENTS.md:579-588`: #102 is a second durable 👍 and the durability paragraph names only one.**
"#99 is the instance to check a year from now" is true but under-counts. #102 is merged (`merged_at` `2026-08-09T05:50:06Z`) with its branch deleted, so its 👍 is frozen on exactly the grounds you give for #99 — "nothing was ever pushed to it again." Failure scenario: the reasoning you want to survive is "a reaction is evidence only until the next push," and it is better carried by two frozen instances than one. Same fix as above, one clause: name #102 alongside #99.

**Should-fix — `AGENTS.md:545-546`: the second push instance does not state the property that disqualifies #99.**
"The next push to the same pull request did it again, ten seconds and three minutes fifty" omits the "with nothing else touched on the pull request" that the first instance states and that the next two sentences use to throw #99 out. Failure scenario: a reader who checks your work cannot tell why one is clean and the other is not, and "two clean instances" rests on cleanliness shown for one. It does hold — I verified it: `pulls/104/reviews` has only `scottdensmore COMMENTED` at `22:30:22Z` and `23:43:28Z`, and `issues/104/comments` is empty, so nothing touched #104 between 22:30:22Z and 23:43:28Z, a window containing both the 23:35:58Z push and the 23:39:48Z 👍. Fix: add the clause, since it is a fact you have and the argument needs it.

**Nit — `AGENTS.md:499-501`: "the ten-second window ... that decides whether a reaction is stale" names the wrong mechanism.**
The ten-second window at lines 606-608 is the *withdrawal latency*, whereas line 605 says staleness is decided by comparing `created_at` — and that comparison is immune to a six-second error in a hand-noted time, because a stale 👍's `created_at` is from a prior cycle, minutes or hours back. The two mechanisms are given the same phrase. The correct reason is already in your next clause and is stronger for being general: a hand-noted lower bound makes any reaction created in that gap read as fresh. Fix: lead with the lower-bound reason and drop or re-anchor the ten-second tie-in.

## Verification of the factual claims (live API)

Reviews: exactly two by `chatgpt-codex-connector[bot]` across all 54 PRs, both `COMMENTED`, on #99 (00:18:32Z) and #104 (05:49:47Z). #102: created `04:39:34Z`, `draft:false`, no `ready_for_review` in its timeline, no review object, 👍 `04:45:13Z` — 5m39s, confirmed. #99: 👍 `00:59:44Z` vs push `00:57:22Z` = 2m22s, and the two `scottdensmore COMMENTED` reviews at `00:57:22Z`/`00:57:23Z` are exactly as described. #104: opened `05:46:08Z` → comments 3m39s; pushes at `06:34:17Z` and `23:35:58Z` in the events feed; current sole reaction 👍 `23:39:48Z` = 3m50s. ## Findings

**1. should-fix — `/home/scottdensmore/Developer/scottdensmore/super-star-trek/AGENTS.md:448`, `:609`, `:626`: the sha anchor does not survive the merge this file mandates.**

You asked whether anchoring to a commit is the right device or just relocates the staleness. The device is right — it converts a claim that goes silently false into one that is permanently true and checkably old, and the reader can settle "still current?" where they could never settle "true?". The defect is the choice of anchor, not the idea. `6ed975b` is a branch commit, and step 12 requires squash merge plus branch deletion. I tested this rather than assumed it: #99's reviewed commit `ac5ebf2754` is resolvable here only because this clone fetched it; a fresh `git clone` of the repository gives `fatal: Not a valid object name ac5ebf2754`. So the moment #104 lands, an agent reading `AGENTS.md` on `main` cannot date any of the three anchored claims in the clone it has — which is precisely the staleness the anchor was added to cure. (GitHub's API still resolves it, so it is not lost everywhere, just where the reader is.) Fix: anchor to something that survives the squash — the date, or `#104`, or both: "up to 2026-08-09 (#104)". That also reads without a lookup.

**2. should-fix — `AGENTS.md:620-622`: the unwatched block misidentifies which 👀 withdrawal was inferred.**

"the entry above dates the 06:34 one from the comments that replaced it" — but no comments ever replaced the 06:34 👀; that one gave way to a 👍 at `06:36:41Z`. The withdrawal the entry above (`:564-569`) dates from comments is the *opening* review's 👀, gone when the comments posted at `05:49:47Z`, three quarters of an hour earlier — which is what that entry says in its own words. A reader reconstructing the timeline from this sentence infers a commenting run around 06:34 that never happened, and it sits inside the one block whose whole job is to keep watched apart from inferred. Fix: "the entry above dates the 05:49 withdrawal from the comments that replaced it". Worth adding that the 06:34 👀's own end at `06:36:41Z` was also polled after the fact — that is a second inferred withdrawal, and naming it strengthens the entry rather than weakening it.

**3. should-fix (low) — `AGENTS.md:633-636`: "Nineteen is the number to plan against" names no action.**

You asked whether the bracketing is actionable. The bracket itself is stated well — 7s and 19s, one run, "not a margin to lean on" — but the imperative under it has nothing to attach to. Follow the paragraph's own first sentence and compare `created_at` against the push, and the withdrawal latency never changes what you do: a 👍 dated `06:36:41Z` reads stale against a `23:35:58Z` push at second 7 exactly as it does at second 700. The number matters only to a reader who checks whether a reaction is *present*, whom the sentence before has just told not to be. As the section's closing line it is the last instruction a reader takes away, and there is no step to take. Fix: state the action the number implies — a reaction read in the first twenty seconds after a push tells you nothing either way, so read its time — or drop the imperative and keep the bracket as a fact about the bot.

**4. nit — `AGENTS.md:486`: "both pushes measured below" is now three.**

The #99 push at `00:57:22Z` is measured below at `:552-553`, and it too shared its second with a push to `entire/checkpoints/v1` (verified in the feed), so the argument holds a fortiori — only the count went stale when the #99 material was added. Fix: "every push measured below shared its second with a push to another branch", which also needs no maintenance the next time a run joins the list.

**5. nit — `AGENTS.md:518-519` contradicts `:552-558` on what triggered #99's 👍.**

The wait paragraph says "#99's push drew a 👍 in 2m22s"; the measured-facts entry says which of the push and the two thread replies that review answered "is not something anyone watched". The duration is unaffected, since all three candidates fall inside one second — it is only the causal verb that overreaches, in a section that elsewhere goes out of its way not to. Fix: "#99 answered 2m22s after the activity at 00:57:22Z".

**6. nit — `AGENTS.md:449-451`: the anchored count says nothing about how to bring it forward.**

The unwatched block is the strong form of the device — every entry names what would settle it. This one is the weak form: true forever, checkably old, and no route to today. The section already gives the reactions call at `:472-474`; the recount needs `gh api repos/:owner/:repo/pulls/&lt;n&gt;/reviews` and one clause saying so.

## Findings

### Should-fix 1 — `/home/scottdensmore/Developer/scottdensmore/super-star-trek/tests/instructions.sh:178-183`: the comment justifying the raise has the history wrong

The comment says the section "tripled between the commit that added this check and the one after it, and 4000 went from a third of the body to under a third of it." Measured:

- `check_section`, both floors and the literal `4000` were all introduced in **6ed975b — the current HEAD**, not an earlier commit (`git log -S'4000' -- tests/instructions.sh` names only 6ed975b).
- At 6ed975b the section body was **7954**; it is **12975** now. That is 1.63×, not tripled.
- 4000 was therefore **50%** of the body when it was written, not "a third". And "from a third of the body to under a third of it" is vacuous as a pair even if the first half were right.

The other reading — that "the commit that added this check" means 33aed5e, which added the *section* — is worse: the body was 3583 there, so the 4000 floor would have failed outright.

Failure scenario: the next maintainer follows the rule this comment sets ("if you find one of these numbers a long way under its section again, that is the thing to fix") and calibrates from a precedent that never happened, concluding a floor at ~33% of its section is the norm when the number actually shipped at 50%.

Fix: state the measured pair — 7954 at the commit that added the floor, 12975 one commit later, 4000 falling from half the body to under a third.

### Should-fix 2 — `/home/scottdensmore/Developer/scottdensmore/super-star-trek/AGENTS.md:594-599`: the 06:34 run did not disprove what this says it did

```
An earlier draft of this section reasoned the
opposite — that a 👍 could not be repeated at all, so the one a pull
request drew on opening would sit there unchanged — which that run
disproved. It left open the narrower question of a 👍 already standing
when the push lands, which is what the run above answered.
```

By this file's own account, #104 carried no reaction at all when the 06:34:17Z push landed — its opening review posted comments at 05:49:47Z and took the 👀 away, and "both commenting runs to that date began from no reaction." So that run produced a 👍 where none stood, which is exactly the entry's heading, and left non-repeatability untested. The next sentence concedes precisely that, so the paragraph asserts and withdraws the same thing three lines apart.

Failure scenario: a reader auditing the watched/inferred/reasoned split — the section's whole reason for existing — finds its most careful passage claiming a premise was disproved by a run that could not touch it, and discounts the rest.

Fix: say the 06:34 run disproved the *conclusion* (that no 👍 could ever postdate your push, which is what step 12's rule needed), and that the premise — one reaction per user per issue, so a standing 👍 is frozen — fell only to the 23:36 run.

### Nit 3 — `/home/scottdensmore/Developer/scottdensmore/super-star-trek/tests/instructions.sh:255`: 8000 is on the right side of the line, but only just, and for a reason the comment does not give

Answering your question directly. 8000/12975 = 62%. Against the sibling in the same function (1200/1680 = 71% for "Answering a finding") that is *looser*, so it is not out of line with your own precedent, and I would not block on the number.

- **8000 exceeds the section's size at HEAD (7954).** The commit immediately behind this one would fail the floor this one ships. That is the concrete sense in which it is nearer a budget than "well under the real size, so ordinary editing never touches it."
- **This section is the file's most volatile, and volatile downward.** The "What was still unwatched" block is 1504 bytes and is written to be pruned — "the first one who does ends this entry." The measured block (3833 bytes) churns with it. Settling two entries and folding them into one-line measured entries is ordinary editing here and could plausibly cost 2000+ bytes.

If 8000 stays, say in the comment that the floor now sits above the size the section had one commit ago, so the next reader is not surprised by how tight it is. If you would rather keep headroom, ~6500 restores the 50% ratio the 4000 floor actually shipped at and still fails any gutting. That does give up the 7476 mutation — but a 42% deletion is a change review sees; the floor is for the silent stub, and the stub fails at either number.

The comment's rule itself is followable as far as it goes, with one gap: nothing ever prompts a maintainer to check it. The floor only speaks when the section *shrinks*, and the condition the comment asks them to notice is the section having *grown*. As written it can only be acted on by someone who happens to read the script.

## 4. Findings

**F1 — medium. The new floor comment's number is already stale in the worktree that carries it.** `tests/instructions.sh:179-181` states the section was "12975" one commit later. It measures **13141** right now, because `AGENTS.md` was edited at 17:44:34 *after* `instructions.sh` at 17:43:28. The ratio argument survives (6500/13141 = 49.5%, still "about half"), but a reader who redoes the measurement gets a different number than the comment claims — which is precisely the drift the comment exists to warn about, recurring inside the fix for it.

**F2 — low. `AGENTS.md:600` states a GitHub constraint that is not one.** "one reaction per user per issue, so a 👍 already standing is frozen where it is". GitHub permits a single user to hold one reaction *of each content type* on the same issue — 👍 and 👀 can coexist. The sentence is framed as the superseded draft's premise, so nothing rests on it, but it reads as a fact about the API. I could not check it empirically here: the repository holds exactly three reactions total, all lone 👍s from the bot.

**F3 — low. `AGENTS.md:490-491`, "The two that filed a `CreateEvent` had their second to themselves".** 04:39:34Z shares its second with a `PullRequestEvent` (#102 opening). No other *push* shares it and the paragraph is about `pushed_at`, so the substance holds; the words are broader than the fact.

**F4 — low. A one-second anchor inconsistency between two paragraphs about the same run.** The timing paragraph gives #104's opening run as 3m39s, which is from the `PullRequestEvent` at 05:46:08Z. The `pushed_at` paragraph counts that run's push as the `CreateEvent` at 05:46:07Z, from which it is 3m40s. Both are internally right; they just do not use the same instant.

**F5 — resolved in the file's favour, not a finding.** I checked whether 6500 could fire on the pruning the section anticipates. Headroom is 6641 characters; the "still unwatched" block is 1503 and the whole measured block is 3998 — deleting *both entirely* leaves 7640, still clear. The comment's "the floor is for the silent stub" is accurate.

## Findings

### should-fix — `/home/scottdensmore/Developer/scottdensmore/super-star-trek/AGENTS.md:474-476`

The reactions recipe never says to filter by the reacting user, so a human 👍 on the pull request satisfies step 12's merge gate.

`gh api repos/:owner/:repo/issues/&lt;n&gt;/reactions` returns every user's reactions. Step 12 (`:320-322`) tells an agent to merge once a 👍 has a `created_at` later than the last push, and the recipe it defers to hands back an unfiltered list whose `content` field reads `+1` whichever account produced it. A maintainer who thumbs-up their own agent's pull request after the final push — an entirely ordinary thing to do, and the more likely the more this workflow is used — produces exactly the shape the gate is looking for, and the merge proceeds with no Codex pass having run at all. The bot's login is named once at `:437`, thirty-seven lines above the recipe, and `:468` says "its own reaction", so a careful reader infers the filter; the section does not leave any of its other traps to inference — it spends `:486-492` on why `pushed_at` cannot be used because it names no branch, and `:481-484` on the `CreateEvent`/`PushEvent` split. This is the same class of trap with a one-clause fix: give the recipe the filter, e.g. `--jq '.[] | select(.user.login == "chatgpt-codex-connector[bot]")'`, or say in the sentence that the 👍 has to be the bot's own.

### nit — `/home/scottdensmore/Developer/scottdensmore/super-star-trek/tests/instructions.sh:185`

The character figure is 13470, not 13471.

`13653` is the byte count and reproduces exactly. The character count of the same body is 13470 — whatever produced 13471 counted one extra record, which is the signature of splitting on `\n` and keeping the empty string after the file's final newline; awk sees 228 records there, not 229. Nothing depends on it (the floor clears both by better than 2000), but the sentence exists to state precisely what the two counts are, in a file whose subject is measured versus assumed. Change `13471` to `13470`.

### nit — `/home/scottdensmore/Developer/scottdensmore/super-star-trek/tests/instructions.sh:270-271`

The `Answering a finding` floor is 71% of its section, against the policy the comment above it just argued for.

The body measures 1680; the floor is 1200. `:173-176` says a floor is "set well under the real size, so ordinary editing never touches it", and `:182` sets the other one at "about half" on that reasoning. Deleting seven lines from a 28-line section — one paragraph of the four — trips this one, and the failure message then invites lowering it, which `:196-197` identifies as how a floor decays into a budget. That section is far more stable than the Codex one, so the risk is small; but the mismatch between the stated policy and this call site is the kind of thing the next reader has to re-derive. Either drop it to ~850 or note beside the call why 71% is right for a section that does not churn.
